Abstract NoteBank augments linear attention models with a sparse episodic memory that stores selected text and replays it through native computation, greatly improving long-context retrieval without extra training or significant overhead.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Linear attention promises the efficiency needed for long-context language modelling, yet its fixed-size recurrent state must simultaneously support ongoing computation and preserve information from an ever-growing history. This coupling has reinforced a prevailing view that the state's memory capacity defines the model's long-context capability. We challenge this presumed ceiling: recurrent computation need not bear the full burden of long-term storage. We introduce NoteBank, a sparse episodic memory that archives selected text before future queries are known, uses the backbone's native features for writing and addressing, and reads retrieved evidence through its native forward computation via replay. This training-free, plug-and-play interface requires no external embedding model, separately trained retriever, or separate neural reader. At one million tokens, it raises Gated DeltaNet's mean accuracy across six RULER retrieval tasks from 1.7% to 70.3%, with no observed significant retrieval degradation from 4K to 1M tokens. This recovery adds leqslant0.025% to analytical FLOPs, with persistent context storage approximately 1/2,600 that of a Transformer's full MHA KV cache at matched width, depth, and precision. Experiments extend from three 1.3B recurrent backbones to Mamba-2-8B and post-trained Qwen3.5-9B, demonstrating gains on LongBench v1/v2 and MemoryAgentBench. Together, these results show that sparse evidence storage and native replay can substantially expand the long-context capability of pretrained linear attention models while retaining the efficiency of recurrent computation.

Abstract Flow matching with self-conditioning enables deeper visual reasoning by allowing iterative sampling steps to increase expressive power beyond constant-depth limits.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Recent work has explored visual reasoning through image generation, where models solve visual-reasoning problems by directly generating solution images. Intuitively, iterative samplers such as flow matching are well suited to this task, since each sampling step may resemble one reasoning step. However, in this work, from a circuit complexity perspective, we show that this intuition is misleading. We prove that as long as the model outputs satisfy certain mild regularity conditions, the trajectory of the vanilla flow-matching sampler can be simulated by a constant number of sequential rounds of parallel model evaluations. Consequently, additional sampling steps cannot increase the expressive power of a constant-depth backbone model under the standard complexity-theoretic model of transformers. We then reveal that self-conditioning, which conditions the model on the previous step's estimate of the final image, plays a previously unrecognized role in removing this bottleneck. Controlled experiments on diverse visual-reasoning tasks show that flow matching with self-conditioning achieves higher accuracy and greater reasoning depth than vanilla flow matching, and its performance improves with more sampling steps. Theoretically, we prove that with self-conditioning, a constant-depth backbone can achieve expressive power equivalent to that of space-bounded Turing machines. Our study suggests a design principle for future visual reasoning research: carry more information between sampling steps, so that additional steps translate into deeper reasoning.

Abstract Next-Concept Modeling uses hierarchical discrete codes and parallel level-wise generation to accelerate diffusion language models while improving quality and reasoning performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer parallel sampling for faster generation than autoregressive models, an increasingly important advantage as inference becomes a dominant computational workload. However, DLMs degrade quickly with fewer sampling steps, process the full sequence at every step, and require many passes over the dataset to reach good quality. We introduce Next-Concept Modeling (NCM), a two-stage, coarse-to-fine framework: a multiscale residual VQ-VAE encodes text into a hierarchy of discrete codes, and a transformer generates one level at a time, predicting all codes within a level in parallel. At matched parameter scales, NCM achieves better generative perplexity than the strongest DLM baseline on LM1B while running 21times faster. On OpenWebText, NCM is competitive with the strongest DLM while running 10times faster; on XSum, it matches the strongest DLM's ROUGE-L while being 31times faster. On the reasoning task of Sudoku, NCM outperforms autoregressive and diffusion baselines across all difficulty levels, using fewer function evaluations. On the reasoning task of Sudoku, NCM outperforms autoregressive and diffusion baselines across all difficulty levels, using fewer function evaluations.

Abstract A vision-language waypoint policy using staged multimodal training improves robot tracking of intended people among distractors and benefits from on-policy refinement.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Embodied visual tracking requires a robot to identify a designated target from visual observations and control its own motion to follow that target through an environment. Following an isolated person is a comparatively simple demonstration of embodied visual tracking, but following the intended person among distractors remains challenging. Existing methods exhibit a substantial drop in success from single-target to distractor tracking, motivating a focus on the shared spaces that person-following robots must navigate. We present , a vision--language waypoint policy built around the hypothesis that visual and language representations should be aligned before tracking-specific motion is learned. The policy combines complementary frozen visual encoders, multiscale temporal context, a transformer backbone, and a lightweight head for robot-centric waypoint prediction. Its training progresses through vision--language alignment, multimodal reasoning mid-training, and tracking specialization on 19,532 oracle episodes. Compared with 41.54\% distractor-tracking success for our earlier OmTrackVLA model, the complete multi-stage recipe achieves 55.80\%. We then explore on-policy refinement using filtered oracle labels for states visited by the policy. This preliminary study adds 2.42 percentage points of distractor-tracking success, reaching 58.22\%, while reducing collision rate by 1.99 pts. The final policy records the highest distractor- and ambiguous-tracking success rates among the compared methods. These results support staged multimodal learning as a promising direction for tracking among distractors and motivate further study of on-policy refinement for person following in shared spaces.

Abstract Drive3R is a block-wise streaming geometric foundation model that enables scalable, long-horizon multi-view 3D reconstruction for autonomous driving by using block-wise causal attention and confidence-aware re-anchoring to reduce drift and maintain global consistency.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small High-fidelity closed-loop simulation for autonomous driving increasingly relies on dense multi-view 3D reconstruction. While recent feed-forward geometric foundation models have revolutionized per-scene optimization, applying them to continuous, long-horizon driving sequences is computationally intractable due to GPU memory limits and the quadratic complexity of global attention. Existing scaling strategies are fundamentally limited, as causal streaming suffers from compounding scale drift and trajectory deviation, while rigid "chunk-then-align" heuristics fragment global context and produce trajectory discontinuities. To address these limitations, we introduce Drive3R, a block-wise streaming geometric foundation model. Its block-wise causal attention resolves the scalability bottleneck of fully bidirectional attention while avoiding the loss of local future context in purely causal streaming. Its confidence-aware re-anchoring further mitigates long-range drift by detecting unreliable streaming states, re-initializing the KV cache, and stitching refreshed segments through SE(3) alignment. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Drive3R establishes a new state-of-the-art on nuScenes, KITTI, and Waymo. Specifically, on nuScenes, it improves over the recent DVGT-2 baseline from 0.1806 to 0.1081 in AbsRel, from 0.6750 to 0.5344 in point-cloud Accuracy, and from 1.5812 to 0.9092 in ATE.

Abstract Cortex validates agent harness updates by replaying developer corrections, retaining only rules and skills that consistently improve performance without degrading tasks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Coding agents such as Claude Code also follow text files that developers write for them: a project file, skills the agent may choose to open, and rules that load automatically when the agent reads matching files. We call these files the agent's harness. Developers add to it whenever they correct the agent, but one success proves little: the same agent can pass a task once and fail it the next time.   We present Cortex, a tool that tests changes to the harness instead of trusting them. It saves fixes from the developer's sessions as replayable tasks, finds the corrections that recur, writes one skill or rule at a time, and keeps it only if repeated runs with and without it show a net gain and no task made noticeably worse. Existing text is tested the same way before it is deleted, and Cortex neither adds nor deletes text the agent never used.   We tested Cortex with Claude Haiku 4.5 on a repository built for the purpose, with conventions the model tends to break, one area with nothing to learn, and a script playing the developer. On held-out tasks, the harnesses it built made the agent follow the conventions far more often than no skills or rules did, left the comparison area unchanged, and added no text read on every turn. Rules, which load automatically, did most of the work; skills helped only when offered with the files a task touches, and those offered on every turn were rarely opened. Cortex kept none of the useless or harmful suggestions we planted, but the agent never opened the useless ones or the harmful skills, and it reached no verdict on the harmful rules, so the test shows that Cortex keeps no unused text, not that it detects damage. Under the stronger Claude Sonnet 5 a single run's harness added little, and on a real open-source project, where the agent was rarely corrected, Cortex found nothing to learn. We release the tool, the lab and its data.

Вместо того чтобы доверять правкам разработчика, Cortex сохраняет исправления как воспроизводимые задачи, находит повторяющиеся поправки и добавляет по одному правилу или навыку, оставляя его только при чистом приросте качества без ухудшения других задач. Abstract Residual-stream forecasting detects unauthorized agent actions and false reports before they occur, enabling low-cost gating that reduces violations and prompt-injection attacks without harming task completion.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Language-model agents sometimes act beyond their authorization and then misreport what they did. On CAPE-Bench, matched agent tasks in which one fact decides whether an action is authorized, frontier open-weight agents do both, and one behavior marks the lie: retroactive permission laundering, acting and then asking permission as if the action were pending, precedes a false report in 70% of 1081 fresh informed violations, against 8% otherwise (preregistered odds ratio 7.0); frontier agents on tau^2-bench instead fabricate the authorization before violating. A linear readout of the agent's residual stream forecasts the violation before the action (AUROC 0.94) and the false report before the report, and beats chain-of-thought monitors when the agent is pressured to hide its reasoning. Gating every proposed action on this forecast cuts violations over whole episodes in four model families, and sending repeated flags to an approver keeps authorized work (Gemma-4: violations 0.70 -> 0.09, completion 1.00; a model approver that escalates unclear cases keeps this gain with a person seeing 25% of asks), at about 10-8 of the agent's compute and 109 times less than a chain-of-thought monitor. On AgentDojo, an external prompt-injection benchmark, the same readout calibrated on 29 tasks, with the steering unchanged, cuts attack success on unseen tasks in two families (Gemma-4 11.7% -> 5.7%, gpt-oss 28.5% -> 17.5%) while keeping benign utility (Gemma-4 0.637 vs 0.637; gpt-oss 0.667 vs 0.681). Preventing the violation is also the most reliable way to prevent the lie (misreported violations 0.10 -> 0.01 pooled over three families, preregistered), whereas lie-specific fixes after a violation are weak. All tests are preregistered and hash-locked; code, data and the full record, including tests that did not pass, are at https://github.com/AryaFayyazi/Honesty.

Abstract A scale mismatch between independently recovered camera and human estimates causes severe world-space drift in fast-motion dance videos, which is corrected by a closed-form rescaling method that stabilizes multi-person reconstruction without retraining.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Monocular world-space human recovery methods excel on current benchmarks, but these rarely include fast camera motion. We introduce the K-Pop stress test, broadcast dance videos that combine fast, large camera motion with dense multi-person choreography. Leading methods produce plausible per-frame meshes on these videos, yet their world-space trajectories drift far from the performers' stage positions. Through a systematic diagnosis, we show that the camera and human estimates each remain self-consistent, and that the drift appears when they are combined at mismatched scales. Each is recovered at its own scale, so a mismatch between the two can turn camera motion into human drift that grows with camera displacement and becomes severe in broadcast dance videos. We derive a closed-form correction that estimates this mismatch and applies one scale to all performers, removing the drift while keeping their relative positions in one shared world frame. It requires no retraining and works with existing backends. Combined with physical constraints, the correction yields stable world-space reconstruction for choreography modeling, scene editing, data generation, and virtual reshooting.
